✅#098🪪One card per person — decided: each card gets a list of ways to reach them2026-08-20
  • SETTLED with Junyan. Booking sites hand out throwaway addresses and phone numbers, and saving those on the card kept minting duplicate cards — 42 cards sit on a throwaway address, one woman has four cards
  • The answer: a new companion table in Notion. Each person's card links to a list of contact entries — what it is (email or phone), where it came from (their real one, or one a booking site made up), and which booking it belongs to
  • That last part was the discovery of the session: a throwaway address is not really the person's address — it is a temporary phone line the platform set up for ONE booking's conversation
  • So the sending rule Junyan set: we generally avoid the throwaway lines — but if the person contacts us through one, we answer on that same line to keep the thread clean. Real email and real phone for everything else
  • Old throwaway lines are never thrown out: when a message arrives from one we have seen before, that is how we recognize 'oh, this is Prajakta again' instead of making a fifth card
  • The Known Emails box (which nothing ever searched) retires — its 19 addresses move into the new table where robots CAN search them
  • Rollout starts with the booking sweep robot; duplicate cards get merge SUGGESTIONS only, a human does every merge
  • One thing we refused to guess: how long each platform keeps a throwaway line alive. That became its own research task (ticket 100)
Technical notes

One card per human — multiple emails, phones and products on a single Artist card

RESOLUTION — 2026-08-20 (grilled with Junyan)

Shape chosen: option 3, a related Contact Points data source — with one refinement

Junyan forced mid-grill: a relay row is scoped to a BOOKING, not just a platform.

The grill surfaced two corrections to the Aug-15 ruling's "latest relay wins" phrasing,

both Junyan's:

  1. 1. Contact points are per-channel, and channels differ in kind. A person's real

email (Skedda) and real phone are stable — person-level, no expiry. Platform relays

(Peerspace email, Giggster proxy phone, Tagvenue) are minted per booking/enquiry and

the relay stays alive for that specific booking; a new booking may mint a new

relay, but the new one does not supersede the old — the old relay is still the right

door for the old booking's thread.

  1. 2. The send rule is therefore not "latest relay wins" globally. Junyan's ruling in

session: *"We generally default away from the relay lines, but if they contact us

through a relay line then we communicate back there to make the thread clean."* So:

  • - Reply in a platform thread → that thread's/booking's own relay row.
  • - Everything else (proactive outreach, invoices, door codes) → stable rows

(real email / real phone) by default; a relay only as last resort.

  • - "Latest relay wins" survives only as the tiebreak within a channel when no

thread context exists.

Row schema: artist (relation) · type (email|phone) · value · source

(skedda|peerspace|giggster|tagvenue|direct) · booking/conversation ref (relays only —

for Giggster, the conversation UUID is the durable key, the proxy number just the current

door) · first_seen / last_seen · status (usable | recognition-only). Rows are never

deleted: an expired relay's permanent job is recognition — matching inbound mail/SMS

to the human so no duplicate card is minted.

What each acceptance item resolved to:

  • - Shape + "latest relay wins" evaluation — as above; the ordering question dissolves

into thread-scoping plus the stable-first default.

  • - Lanes — rollout is booking_sweep first (Junyan approved), other lanes follow as

091 reaches them. The lookup becomes "any Contact Points row with this value" instead

of Email exact-match.

  • - Per-platform relay lifecycle is UNKNOWN and must not be guessed — when a relay

dies after its booking closes differs by platform (Tagvenue entirely unverified).

Junyan: "each platform has different situations so we need a full investigation."

Graduated as 100; it tunes the

usable→recognition-only flip, blocks nothing structural.

  • - Known Emails — retired into the new shape: its 19 cards' addresses migrate to

Contact Points rows (source direct unless provably platform), then the field stops

being treated as a lookup surface.

  • - Migration (42 relay-primary cards, 25 duplicate names) — proposals only, merges

stay human-only; the Contact Points table is built and back-filled first so each

proposed merge can show its evidence (shared stable contact or shared conversation

UUID). Reconciling the 19 duplicate-code pairs against the 25 duplicate names happens

inside that proposal pass. Execution is follow-on work coordinated with

091, not this ticket.

  • - Giggster SMS parsing — the fixed message shape (guest name + conversation UUID +

proxy number) writes a complete Contact Points row from the existing SMS poller.

Question

Junyan ruled this on 2026-08-15 and it was never built. What SHAPE do multi-valued

contact points take in the Artist DB, and how do 1,040 live rows migrate onto it

without breaking the lanes that read them?

The ruling (already made — do not re-litigate the principle)

Next Action row 3b97503281c48167a742c189836aa19d,

status Ready, rewritten by Junyan in the 2026-08-15 review walk:

One Artist card per human: the NAME is the identity. Each card carries multiple
connected emails and multiple connected phone numbers; the platform relay addresses and
any new numbers attach to the existing card rather than minting a new one. Current send
address = the most recent working contact (latest relay wins).

Its own note says: *"Execution is a schema + migration project: plan it before touching

live records."* That is what this ticket is.

Extended 2026-08-19 (Junyan, in session): PRODUCTS are multi-valued too — one card

should hold more than one product rather than splitting a person across cards. Product

is already a multi_select, so this half may be mostly a data/usage question rather than

a schema one — confirm before assuming work is owed.

Why it matters (the mechanism, measured 2026-08-20)

Tagvenue, Peerspace and Giggster mint a fresh reply-relay email per enquiry. The desk

must use the current relay to reach the renter through the platform, so it cannot be

discarded — but it lands in the card's Email field, so every repeat booking from the

same human mints a new card. The Aug-15 note calls this class unbounded, and unlike

the private-vs-group duplicate class (bounded at one extra card per person) it grows

forever.

Live counts against the Artist DB (1,040 rows) on 2026-08-20:

| Measure | Count |

|---|---|

| Live cards whose primary email is a platform relay address | 42 |

| Names holding more than one live card | 25 |

| ...of which at least one card is a relay address | 9 |

| Cards with a second email parked in Known Emails | 19 |

Worst offenders: prajakta dravid 4 cards, devanshi mishra 3,

angelica gil aguilar 3. Jai Smith was 3 and was merged to 1 on 2026-08-20; that

merge is a symptom being treated by hand, not the fix.

The finding that makes the current workaround a lie

Known Emails is not a lookup key. Nothing searches it. booking_sweep.find_artist

queries exactly three things — Skedda User ID, then Email (exact), then Name (exact

title). Verified in source 2026-08-20.

So the standing habit of "add the second address to Known Emails" records the fact for a

human reader and changes nothing about whether any lane can find that person by it.

19 cards currently rely on that field. Three more were added on 2026-08-20 during the 085

review (Nicole Drury, Quynh Tran, Alison Murray) knowing this — they are notes, not

fixes, and this ticket is what makes them real.

What ticket 085 already de-risked

Id-first matching is live: the sweep matches Skedda User ID before email. So for a

person who keeps ONE Skedda account, the relay-email churn no longer breaks the join —

the id carries it. That shrinks this problem but does not remove it, because:

  • - the id is only present once someone has been stamped (167 of 702 coded rows), and
  • - a person who books through two platforms can still surface as two Skedda venueusers,
  • - and the alarm side still has no equivalent for a card that was never matched.

The phone axis — same disease, measured 2026-08-20

Everything above is written about relay email addresses. Giggster does the identical

thing to phone numbers, and that was not known when this ticket was drafted.

On 2026-08-14 Junyan pointed the Giggster host account's chat notifications at the desk

line (+1 647-932-1050) so the SMS poller would capture platform correspondence. Six days

of real traffic show Giggster does not have a sender number — it has a pool:

| Number | Carries |

|---|---|

| +1 365-645-5150 · +1 289-804-8305 | transactional (verification, booking confirmed/cancelled, review request) |

| +1 249-700-0058 | new-inquiry notices |

| +1 226-773-9040 | Hanano I.'s chat thread |

| +1 226-773-9212 | Dhan P.'s chat thread |

The 226-773-xxxx numbers are per-conversation proxies, one per guest thread — the

phone-side twin of "a fresh reply-relay email per enquiry". Same unbounded growth, same

"which address do we use now?" question, and the same trap: a proxy that gets recycled

after a booking closes will reach a different guest, so a stored number without a

last-seen timestamp is not merely stale, it is a mis-delivery risk.

Two things this changes:

It settles the shape argument. A Giggster SMS field (or Known Phones, option 1)

was proposed in session on 2026-08-20 and is withdrawn — it is option 1, and option 1

cannot evaluate "latest relay wins" because it stores no ordering. With churn now proven

on both the email and phone axes, option 3's type | source | is_current | last_seen

row is doing real work on two axes rather than one.

The raw material is already parseable. Every Giggster relay text has a fixed shape:

``

(Giggster) Hanano I.:

Hi ! You guys have a speaker ??

Reply via text or at https://giggster.com/messages/bd56c05f-...-4ffa929c44ff?channel=sms

`

That is a guest name, a conversation UUID, and the current proxy number in one

message — i.e. a complete Contact Points row, already arriving on a line we poll. The

conversation UUID is the durable key; the proxy number is only the current door to it.

Whatever shape wins should store both, and prefer the UUID as identity.

Reply-by-text is confirmed working, so once a person is resolvable, outbound platform

correspondence is captured by the existing SMS poller with no scraper.

Shipped meanwhile, and deliberately narrow (dc-canon 0eec2249): event-gate now drops

platform relay SMS before actuation, matching the platform-owned prefix rather than a

number list — a static list could never cover a per-guest pool, and the seeded list had

guarded only the harmless transactional numbers while leaving every guest-word channel

open. That closes a live hazard (a guest typing "there's water everywhere" into a chat

about a booking weeks out classified as forward/water and would have summoned on-call,

since actuator.actuate summons regardless of watchlist). It does not touch identity:

the correspondence still lands attributed to a proxy number, which is this ticket's job.

Shape options to grill (none chosen)

  1. 1. Sibling text field — add Known Phones to mirror Known Emails, and teach every

lane to query both. Cheapest; keeps the "one wide row" model; parsing free text as a

lookup key is exactly the fragility this effort has been removing.

  1. 2. Multi-select / rich_text list with a parse contract — structured-ish, still one row,

still string parsing, but a documented separator makes queries honest.

  1. 3. A related Contact Points data source — one row per email/phone, typed

(email|phone), with is_current, source (skedda/tagvenue/peerspace/direct) and

last_seen. Properly relational, makes "latest relay wins" a query rather than a

convention, and gives the send lanes one place to ask "how do I reach this person now?".

Most work, most durable.

Whatever wins must answer: which address does an outbound message use? — for

phones as well as emails, per the phone-axis section above. The ruling says

latest working contact, latest relay wins — that is a rule about ordering, so the shape

has to store enough to evaluate it (a timestamp or an ordering), which option 1 does not.

Constraints

  • - Fill-only still holds — never blank a populated Alarm Display Name or Alarm Code

(the 081 rule).

  • - Merging two cards is irreversible and stays a human's call; the migration proposes, it

does not auto-merge. The Same Person relation already exists and is unused — consider it.

  • - 19 known duplicate-code pairs were measured 2026-08-10; reconcile that count with the 25

duplicate names above before migrating, they are probably overlapping views of one set.

  • - Coordinate with 091: both touch identity resolution,

and 091's code_mirror work will be reading these same rows.

Acceptance

  • - A shape is chosen and written down with its reasons, including how "latest relay wins" is

evaluated — and it covers relay PHONE numbers as well as relay emails.

  • - Every lane that resolves a person is updated to query it — at minimum booking_sweep,

and whatever 091 leaves name-keyed.

  • - The 42 relay-primary cards and 25 duplicate names have a migration plan; execution may be

staged, but no card is auto-merged without a human.

  • - Giggster chat relays are parsed into whatever the shape is (guest name + conversation

UUID + current proxy number), so platform correspondence attaches to the human rather

than to a proxy number.

  • - Known Emails`' status is settled: promoted to a real key, or retired into the new shape.

It must not remain a field people believe is searched when it is not.

✅#099🗂️The Registrar queues bodyless confirmations — and the confirmation is not actually templatable2026-08-20
Technical notes

The Registrar queues bodyless confirmations — and the confirmation is not actually templatable

Question

queue_confirmation() in dc-canon/services/event-gate/booking_sweep.py:490 writes Message

Code, Channel, Status, Ticket Type, Studio, Artist and Reasoning — and no Message Body.

081 specified the confirmation as "strictly templated"; the

template never reached the code.

Today that is harmless — the rows sit at Pending Review and the send engine ignores them.

Tomorrow it is a trap: a reviewer flips one to Ready to Send and the engine fails it with

"Message Body is empty", and a bodyless review row makes review meaningless — there is

nothing to approve. Four live rows are already in this state (Aug 21 19:00 Amandeep;

Aug 22 08:00 + 10:00 Tufan; Aug 22 12:00 Emma; Aug 22 16:30 Aris).

The template does exist in canon — two rows, not one, in the How to Respond DB

(collection://b0c59a82-0a2b-4e96-9467-89bbd954d8b4):

  • - Booking confirmation — Skedda booking accepted + paid (T2) — first-time renters, full onboarding boilerplate.
  • - Booking confirmation — returning renter (short) — returning renters; body is

Hi [Name], you're all set for [Day, Date] — Studio [###], [start–end] 🙏 + a conditional

terms paragraph + Looking forward to having you back!

So the real question is not "what does it say" but "can code say it at all". Reading the

returning-renter row's Notes, composing this message correctly requires three judgments the

Registrar deliberately has no LLM to make:

  1. 1. First-time vs returning — which of the two cards applies (renter history).
  2. 2. Gate 1, account type (Junyan, 2026-08-05) — agreement renters (Time Slot / Fixed

Option, AAP / 1AP / Private Class, Credit hours, Platform) must get *"Please refer to your

agreement for exact terms & conditions…"* instead of the stored no-refund/24h paragraph,

which is simply wrong for them. Requires reading Product / Account Status on the Artist DB.

  1. 3. Gate 2, recency (one-off renters only) — include the terms paragraph only if they have

not had it in ~30 days. Requires reading threads/<person>.md.

Plus variants the card carries: same-day-additional block, and multi-date (one greeting, one

closer, one line per booking) — the multi-date case is live right now, Tufan has two blocks

on Aug 22.

Decide, then implement:

  • - (a) Deterministic subset. Code emits only the safe core — greeting + booking line(s),

no terms paragraph ever — and the reviewer adds terms when they apply. Cheapest; makes the

review row real; risks the terms silently never being sent.

  • - (b) Deterministic + mechanical gates. Gate 1 is a property read (Product / Account

Status) and Gate 2 is a date scan of a thread file — both are arguably rails, not judgment.

Code could do all three branches. More work, and it moves policy wording into the service,

which fights the one-copy rule (canon owns the words, not the code).

  • - (c) Hand the composition off. The Registrar queues the row with a body-shaped

placeholder plus the card link, and a judgment lane (Responder / Concierge) fills it.

Keeps the Registrar LLM-free and canon single-copy; adds a second actor to the chain.

  • - (d) Don't queue confirmations at all — the Registrar stops at prerequisites and the

existing message lane handles the confirmation. Narrows 081's scope.

Note the same function also omits Raised by: AI (automation), which cards.py

write_queue_row does set, and sets no Email Subject (harmless while Channel is hard-coded

SMS — but the hard-code is itself unexamined for renters with no mobile).

Whatever is decided: the code fix ships as an event-gate redeploy

(gcloud run deploy event-gate --source services/event-gate from the dc-canon root — see

RUNTIMES.md for the path gotcha), and the four existing rows need backfilling before anyone

reviews them. Both touch customer-message content, so drafts go to Junyan before anything

flips.

---

Rulings (Junyan, 2026-08-20) — option (b), the gates are rails

The terms line is one line for everyone this lane confirms. The branch is on the

BOOKING, not the renter:

  • - A standing/recurring slot is never confirmed. No new code was needed — selection_of

already drops recurring series ("recurring series — out of scope, one-shot lane only"),

and that skip is why agreement renters' slots were never confirmed.

  • - Everything else is a one-off, including an agreement renter's EXTRA bookings, which

follow the one-off rules. So they get the standard line, not the refer-to-your-agreement

pointer — that pointer belongs to bookings the agreement actually governs, which this

lane never sends.

The wording says what the renter CAN do, which the stored no-refund paragraph did not:

Just a reminder that you can rebook with at least 24 hours' notice, and a booking can be
moved up to one month out.

Gated at 90 days (Junyan raised it from the cards' 30). The check is a Message Queue

read, not a threads/<person>.md read — the queue is the record of what the desk actually

sent and it is reachable from the cloud. Fails closed: an unreadable queue suppresses the

reminder rather than repeating it. First-time confirmations always carry the line; a

first-timer has by definition never been told.

AGREEMENT_PRODUCTS = 1AP, 2AP, AAP, Flex Option, Fixed Option, Private Class

Add-on — 2AP and Flex Option are retired products whose holders are still on signed

agreements. It no longer decides wording, but the set is recorded because it is the honest

answer to "who is on an agreement".

THE BIGGER DEFECT — found while checking the gate against live data

All four rows were duplicates. Every one of those bookings had already been confirmed

by hand, days before the sweep queued anything:

| Booking | Already sent | |

|---|---|---|

| Amandeep Kaur, Aug 21 | Aug 18 — "Booking confirmation (returning, short)" | Sent |

| Aris Wu, Aug 22 | Aug 19 — one text covering Aug 22 and Sep 11 | Sent |

| Emma Biron, Aug 22 | Aug 19 — first-time T2 | Sent |

| Tufan Bhattarai, Aug 22 | Aug 7 — inside a booking-move acknowledgement | Sent |

confirmation_queued() matched only its own BC-sweep- Message Code, so it could not see

a confirmation any other lane had sent. Backfilling bodies would have made four duplicate

confirmations releasable — the missing body was hiding the worse bug, not causing it.

Note Emma: she was a genuine first-timer (the Aug 19 send was the full T2), but her Artist

record already existed, so the approved "record created this pass ⇒ first-time" rule would

have read her as returning. The rule stands, but this is its known failure mode.

Done in code (unshipped)

  • - compose_confirmation() renders both canon cards; no row can be queued without a body.
  • - terms_sent_recently() — the 90-day gate, fails closed.
  • - confirmation_queued() widened to a second rung: any non-declined queue row for this

renter within 45 days naming the booking's date in either month spelling ("August 22" /

"Aug 22") and reading like a confirmation. Deliberately biased toward suppressing —

a false hit costs a confirmation the booking probably already had, a miss costs the

renter a duplicate text.

  • - merge_contiguous() — touching blocks in one studio on one day are ONE session and one

text. Tufan's 08:00–10:00 + 10:00–12:00 was about to send two.

  • - Raised by: AI (automation) now set, matching cards.py.
  • - 372 tests green across event-gate.

Resolution — 2026-08-20, all three approved by Junyan

  1. 1. Deployed. event-gate-00062-24j. Verified properly rather than assumed: /health

reports code_fingerprint d33cc28c198e, byte-identical to local, so the running code is

this code. Env block intact — 18 vars on both the new and prior revision, nothing

dropped, BOOKING_SWEEP_DISABLED=0. (Note: RUNTIMES.md says to expect 19 env vars

after a deploy; the true count is 18 on both sides of this deploy, so that number is

stale and worth correcting there.)

  1. 2. All five live rows set to Will Not Send, each carrying its own reason on the row.

Not backfilled — every one of those bookings was already confirmed by hand.

  1. 3. Both HTR cards filed as suggestions in 🔧 Sweep Feedback for Junyan to apply

(robots do not edit the How to Respond DB):

  • - returning-renter card — 3 edits
  • - first-time T2 card — wording + the branch collapses

Until those two cards are applied, the code and the humans disagree — the Registrar

sends the new line, a person composing by hand reads the old card. That gap is tracked in

Sweep Feedback, not here.

✅#100📞Found out how long each booking site's throwaway contact lines stay alive2026-08-20
  • Done on August 20, and we did it by reading four months of our own message history — nobody had to send a test message to anyone
  • We thought each throwaway line belonged to one booking and died with it. Both email sites turned out to work differently
  • Peerspace hands out a new address more often than once per booking — one guest got two seventeen minutes apart — and then those addresses keep working for a MONTH after the booking is over. One is still live 33 days on
  • Tagvenue makes a new address every time someone asks about a space, even if they never book. One guest has three, another has four
  • Good news: neither email site can ever hand an old address to a different person. Peerspace stamps the time into the address and Tagvenue stamps a number that is never used twice, so an old one always points back at the right human
  • That leaves the worry on Giggster alone. Its text-message numbers say nothing about who is on the other end, and they come from a small pool
  • The sharpest thing we learned: Giggster texts come from TWO kinds of number, and they look alike. One kind is a real guest. The other is Giggster's own robot sending booking notices, cancellations, and even our own login code — about different guests, all from one number. Save that as somebody's phone number and you have stapled Giggster the company onto a dancer's card
  • And a quiet trap: if we ever text a line that has gone dead, nothing bounces. Not one bounce in four months. The message simply vanishes and it looks exactly like the guest ignoring us — so the rules we wrote go by age, never by waiting for an error
  • One thing we deliberately did NOT do: text an old Giggster number to see what happens. Texting a number that may already belong to a stranger IS the accident we are trying to prevent. Instead we cut a new job (ticket 102) that just watches and tells us the day it happens
  • Also spotted on the way: Tagvenue warns that forwarding one of its emails can let the reader into our account. So we save the address, and we never forward the mail
Technical notes

Platform relay lifecycle — when does each platform's relay actually die?

RESOLUTION — 2026-08-20 (ledger evidence, no test sends)

Answered from the correspondence ledger alone — no controlled test-reply was needed or

sent. 6,369 ledger rows plus 599 compiled threads (Apr 17 → Aug 20) carry enough relay

traffic to settle all four questions for the two email platforms, and to settle the

shape of the SMS question for Giggster while leaving its one genuinely open number

(recycling interval) to a zero-risk passive detector rather than a guess or a probe.

The headline correction: relays are not per-booking. The charting assumption (from

098, Junyan 2026-08-20) was that a relay is minted per

booking and dies with it. The ledger says otherwise on both email platforms, in opposite

directions — Peerspace mints more often than per-booking on the way in and keeps them

alive far longer than the booking on the way out; Tagvenue mints per enquiry, which

is upstream of a booking and can exist without one.

Peerspace — prod-<8 hex><16 hex>@reply.peerspace.com

  1. 1. Minting: per notification thread, not per booking and not per guest. Jessica T. has

two live addresses minted 17 minutes apart on Jul 24 (prod-6a638fac… 16:15Z,

prod-6a6393a4… 16:32Z), both used the same afternoon on the same booking

conversation. Lidia A. has prod-6a6907ea… (Jul 30) and prod-6a7df4b8… (Aug 13).

The first 8 hex digits are a Unix epoch — decode them and you get the mint time to

the minute, which is how the per-thread pattern above is provable rather than inferred

(0x6a638fac = 2026-07-24 16:15 UTC, 42 minutes before our first send to it).

  1. 2. Expiry: no observed expiry. Anneka K.'s prod-6a5b8fab… (minted Jul 18) was still

carrying traffic Aug 20 — 33 days. Angelica B.'s prod-6a6621c3… (Jul 26) likewise

ran to Aug 20 — 25 days. Both spans cover multiple completed bookings. A Peerspace

relay outliving its booking by a month is the normal case, not the edge.

  1. 3. Recycling: structurally impossible. The embedded mint epoch means a reissued address

would have to carry a stale timestamp. No recycling risk on this platform.

  1. 4. Failure signature: none — silent. See the cross-platform finding below.

Tagvenue — first_last-u<N>@reply.tagvenue.com

  1. 1. Minting: per enquiry. N is a global monotonic counter across all Tagvenue

users, not a user id — it rises strictly with calendar date across unrelated people

(u2779836 Apr 19 → u2978834 Aug). Repeat guests get a fresh one every time: Rita Stefan

three (u2814814 Apr 21, u2855029 May 18, u2905754 Jun 24), Jai Smith four,

Carly Ream-Neal, Claire Lee, Sara A., Paula Astardjian and Tasneem Chaudhary two each.

Tasneem's two are four days apart (u2831971 May 2, u2836534 May 6) — so this is an

enquiry counter, and an enquiry is not a booking.

  1. 2. Expiry: not observed, but an enquiry-level clock exists. The longest arc is Vipul

Vijayanand Pandit's u2863302, live 19 days (May 25 → Jun 13) across enquiry →

booking → invoice. Tagvenue's own notification body states *"This enquiry automatically

expires on…"* — 4 days out on Rita's Jun 24 enquiry. Whether the address dies with the

enquiry is unproven; every send we ever made to a Tagvenue relay was inside the

enquiry arc, so the ledger cannot see past it.

  1. 3. Recycling: structurally impossible — the address carries the guest's own name plus

a never-reused monotonic id.

  1. 4. ⚠️ Security note, not asked for but load-bearing for 101:

every Tagvenue notification carries *"Forwarding this email could allow the recipient to

log into your Tagvenue account."* The relay address is credential-adjacent. Storing the

address in the Contact Points table is fine; forwarding the notification email is not.

Giggster — two number classes, and only one of them is a person

The single most consequential finding of this ticket: Giggster SMS traffic arrives on two

kinds of number, and treating them alike mis-files a platform robot as a customer.

  • - Guest conversation relays — +1 226-773-9040 (Hanano I.), +1 226-773-9212

(Dhan P.), +1 249-700-0058 (Kiah F.). Body always opens (Giggster) <Name>: and always

closes with https://giggster.com/messages/<uuid>?channel=sms. The UUID is the durable

key and the number is a leased door — exactly as 098

ruled, now confirmed in the wild.

  • - Platform notification senders — +1 289-804-8305, +1 365-645-5150. These carry

booking confirmations, cancellations, review requests and our own Giggster verification

code, about different guests, with no name prefix and no UUID. +1 365-645-5150 sent

a login code on Aug 14 and a booking confirmation on Aug 17. A contact point minted

from one of these numbers would attach the platform itself to some artist's card —

a live mis-filing hazard that already exists in the data.

  1. 1. Minting: per conversation, from a pool (four distinct Ontario area codes seen:

226 / 249 / 289 / 365).

  1. 2. Expiry: unknown. Every guest relay observed is a single 1–3 day burst.
  2. 3. Recycling: unproven, and the only real recycling risk on any platform. The number

carries no identity whatsoever, so nothing structural prevents reuse — unlike both email

relays. Observation window is only ~3 weeks (SMS canon starts 2026-07-31) with 3 guest

relays — far too thin to see a reuse cycle. The platform-notification numbers already

demonstrate that one Giggster number happily serves many guests' business, so

number→person is provably not injective on this platform even before recycling.

  1. 4. Failure signature: none.

Cross-platform: expiry is invisible from our side

Zero bounces from any relay domain in four months. The entire ledger contains exactly

two mailer-daemon messages — no-reply@phone.com (Jun 25) and a typo'd gmail address

(May 2). Not one from reply.peerspace.com, reply.tagvenue.com or reply.giggster.com.

So the desk cannot detect relay expiry passively. Any usable → recognition-only flip

must be driven by age and supersession, never by a delivery error — and a send to a dead

relay is a silent loss, which is the failure mode that looks like a guest ignoring us.

The flip rules, one line each (ready to encode in 101)

  • - Peerspace — usable while it is the newest prod- row for that artist; demote to

recognition-only when a newer prod- row exists for the same artist and this one

has had no traffic for 30 days (the 33-day Anneka span sets that floor, so a shorter

window would retire live addresses).

  • - Tagvenue — usable only while its enquiry/booking arc is live; demote to

recognition-only the moment a newer -u<N> row appears under the same name prefix, or

14 days after the booking ends, whichever comes first.

  • - Giggster — usable only while this number's most recent inbound carries the

conversation UUID you are replying to, and that inbound is under 7 days old;

otherwise recognition-only. Never key a send on the number alone.

  • - Giggster platform numbers — a fourth state, not a relay at all: blocklist

+12898048305 and +13656455150 (and any Giggster-sender number whose body has no

(Giggster) <Name>: prefix) from ever minting a contact point.

Recycling risk — flagged loudly

Both email platforms are recycle-proof by construction (mint epoch / monotonic id).

Giggster SMS is not, and a recycled proxy reaches the wrong person. Do not treat the

absence of an observed collision as safety — the window is three weeks and three guests.

Deliberately did NOT run the ticket's controlled test-reply. Sending to a possibly

recycled Giggster number is the mis-delivery we are trying to prevent, and it would still

not measure the interval. Graduated 102

instead: log (number, conversation UUID, guest name, timestamp) on every Giggster inbound

and alarm the first time one number carries a second UUID. Zero outbound risk, no approval

needed, and it measures the real interval instead of proving a single point.

Acceptance

  • - ✅ Written lifecycle per platform, each line backed by named addresses and dates.
  • - ✅ Flip rule per platform, one line each.
  • - ✅ Recycling risk flagged — and narrowed from "all three platforms" to Giggster SMS alone,

with the two email platforms cleared on structural grounds rather than on silence.

  • - ⚠️ Two gaps left honest rather than guessed: whether a Tagvenue relay dies with its

enquiry (never observed past the arc), and the Giggster recycling interval (window too

short). Neither blocks 101 — both flip rules above

are already conservative in the direction of the unknown.

Question

Graduated from 098. The Contact Points shape stores a

usable | recognition-only status on every relay row, and the flip rule is per-platform —

but the actual lifecycles are unverified. Junyan, 2026-08-20: relays are minted per

booking and stay alive for that booking; beyond that, *"each platform has different

situations so we need a full investigation."*

For each of Peerspace (email relay), Giggster (email + SMS proxy), and

Tagvenue (unverified entirely), establish:

  1. 1. Is a fresh relay minted per booking/enquiry, or does one persist per guest?
  2. 2. Does the relay keep working after the booking completes? For how long?
  3. 3. Is a dead relay ever recycled to a different guest? (Giggster's 226-773-xxxx SMS

pool makes this a live mis-delivery risk, not a theoretical one — see 098's phone-axis

section.)

  1. 4. What does a send to a dead relay look like from our side — bounce, silent drop, or

platform error? (Determines whether the desk can detect expiry passively.)

Method (cheap, observational first)

  • - Mine the correspondence ledger: for each platform, find guests with multiple bookings

and check whether the relay address/number changed between them, and whether any

thread continued on an old relay after its booking closed.

  • - Where the ledger is inconclusive, one controlled test-reply per platform to an old

relay on a closed booking (harmless content, e.g. a thank-you) — Junyan approves

each send per the outbound-message rule.

  • - Record findings per platform in this ticket; the output is the flip rule each

platform's rows use in the Contact Points table.

Acceptance

  • - A written lifecycle per platform (minting, expiry, recycling, failure signature),

each backed by ledger evidence or a dated test — no guesses.

  • - The usable → recognition-only flip rule for each platform stated in one line each,

ready to encode.

  • - Any recycling risk found is flagged loudly (a recycled proxy reaches the WRONG person).
✅#101🔌Build the contact list table and teach the robots to search it2026-08-20
  • This is the building job for the decision made on August 20: one card per person, with a list of ways to reach them
  • Three steps. Make the new table in Notion. Copy in every email and phone we already have on all 1,040 cards, including the 19 spare addresses sitting in the box nothing ever searched
  • Then change how the booking robot finds people: today it looks for one exact email address, and after this it looks for ANY address or number we have ever seen for that person
  • That one change is what stops the duplicate cards — an old throwaway address from a past booking now points at the person who used it instead of looking like a stranger
  • It also teaches us to read Giggster's chat texts properly: each one already contains the guest's name, a permanent conversation number, and the temporary phone line, which is everything a contact entry needs
  • Nothing gets merged in this job and nothing gets deleted — it only adds. Joining duplicate cards together is a separate job, and a person does every single one
  • The robot change goes out the careful way that worked last time: put it up, run one practice pass, read what it says it would do, and only then let it run on its own
Technical notes

Build the Contact Points table and move the person lookup onto it

RESOLUTION — 2026-08-20

Built and proven live. The desk now finds a renter by ANY of their contact

points; the deploy of the lookup is the one piece left for Junyan.

The table. ☎️ Contact Points

(data source cbf5b8aa-f836-4fee-aa1f-d0da48c4fda2), under Source Pages

(Dance Annex), with the 098 schema plus one addition made in the build:

Guest Label — the platform's display name for a guest ("Hanano I."). It

earns its place because a relay row arrives with no identity attached, and a

partial name is a hint for the human who links the card, never a lookup key.

Two views: Switchboard (by person) (grouped by Artist — the human-readable

surface 098 asked for) and Relays needing a person (Artist is empty — the

work queue the Giggster lane feeds).

Normalization, decided once: emails stripped and lowercased; phones E.164,

with +1 assumed only for a bare 10-digit NANP number. Invisible characters

(directional marks, NBSP) are stripped first — a valid UK number was

unplaceable until they were. Anything that still cannot be placed is

reported, never guessed.

Back-fill: 1,709 rows from 1,051 live Artist rows — 1,663 direct,

27 tagvenue, 19 peerspace, zero unnormalized. Script:

dc-canon/scripts/contact_points_backfill.py (additive only, re-runnable,

--dry-run / --verify).

Ruling made in the build: the classifier labels a row by SENDING DOMAIN

only, so the ~700 addresses that came in through Skedda are direct, not

skedda. skedda and direct are both stable rows and behave

identically in the send rule, so the mislabel costs nothing while guessing a

platform costs a wrong door. Rows the sweep writes from now on stamp skedda,

because the sweep knows where it read them.

Coverage verified twice, per the workspace's own warning about transient SQL

omissions: the script's independent re-read says 1,709 present / 0 to create,

and a Notion aggregate over the table independently returns 1,709 in the same

four buckets.

The lookup moved. find_artist's email rung is now "any Contact Points row

whose Value matches"; the id rung is untouched and first, the name rung

untouched and last. The legacy Email exact query survives as a fallback

for a card created between the back-fill and its first contact-point write —

it decays on its own, because every card the sweep creates now gets its

contact points at creation (record_contact_point).

Proven on the live board, not in a mock:

| door | resolves to |

|---|---|

| tr.quynhtran@gmail.com (Skedda) | 38b75032…04289 |

| prod-6a3e064f…@reply.peerspace.com (old relay) | 38b75032…04289 |

| the same relay in MIXED CASE | 38b75032…04289 |

| jthadickal1994@gmail.com / its Peerspace relay | 3a775032…f6852 both |

| an address nobody has | ('', 'missing') — still creates, never mis-matches |

Quynh Tran is one of the three cards added to Known Emails on 2026-08-20

"knowing this changes nothing". It is now real.

Dry-run over the live board (162 scanned · 122 skipped · 7 deferred ·

residue 8 · errors 0): every booking resolved artist:found except one —

alice hamman, correctly artist:ambiguous, because she genuinely holds two

cards sharing one email. That is the merge class, human-only by 098. All 8

residue rows were existing; the change created none. (The

access:check-failed lines on same-day bookings are the alarm panel being

unreachable from a laptop, not a code defect.)

Giggster SMS → a row. app.parse_giggster_relay reads the fixed guest-chat

shape into {value, type, source, booking_ref, guest_label} and

booking_sweep.record_relay_contact_point files it with no Artist attached

and a dedup key of (value, booking_ref) — a relay is scoped to a BOOKING, so

the same proxy reused for a new conversation is a new contact point.

Transactional Giggster notices yield nothing. Both are best-effort: an SMS is

never failed because Notion was slow.

Known Emails is retired — nothing read it (verified in 098), and it is

renamed on the card to Known Emails (retired → Contact Points) so the habit

stops rather than relying on someone remembering.

The event-gate redeploy. The lookup is committed but the cloud service

still runs the old code, so the live sweep still matches on Email alone.

Deploying is a production flip and 082's order applies: deploy → dry-run read

→ leave unattended. The dry-run above already exercised the new code against

the live board from this machine and was clean, so the deploy is the remaining

step, not a re-investigation.

Also worth knowing: this session's work was twice wiped out of the working

tree by a concurrent session's git stash/checkout in dc-canon, and an

unrelated commit swept in another session's in-flight ticket-099 changes. No

work was lost — it was recovered and pushed — but two agents editing one file

in this repo is a real hazard, not a fluke.

Question

098 chose the shape and the send rule. This ticket

builds it: create the Contact Points data source in Notion, back-fill it from the 1,040

live Artist rows, and make booking_sweep.find_artist resolve a person through it instead

of through a single Email exact match.

The shape and the rules are settled — do not re-litigate them here. Read 098's

resolution block first; this ticket only executes it.

The shape (from 098, for reference while building)

One row per contact point, related to an Artist row:

| Property | Type | Notes |

|---|---|---|

| Artist | relation → Artist DB (3cfb93cb-55f9-46a4-bde8-9b7023797d4b) | the human this reaches |

| Type | select: email \| phone | |

| Value | rich_text (normalized) | the address or number itself — the lookup key |

| Source | select: skedda \| peerspace \| giggster \| tagvenue \| direct | |

| Booking Ref | rich_text | relays only: booking id, or Giggster's conversation UUID (the durable key — the proxy number is only the current door) |

| First Seen / Last Seen | date | |

| Status | select: usable \| recognition-only | flip rule per platform comes from 100; until it reports, nothing auto-flips |

Stable rows (skedda, direct) carry no Booking Ref and never expire.

Build order

1. Create the data source and its views. One row per contact point; a view grouped by

Artist is the human-readable "switchboard" surface. Normalization must be decided once and

written down here (lowercase emails; phone numbers to E.164) — the lookup is only as good

as the normalization, and a mismatch here silently reintroduces duplicate cards.

2. Back-fill from the 1,040 live rows. Every existing Email becomes a row; every

existing phone becomes a row; the 19 Known Emails cards are promoted (source direct

unless the address is provably a platform relay). Classification is mechanical — a relay

address matches the platform's own sending domain — and anything the classifier cannot

place goes direct and is listed for a human rather than guessed at. Back-fill is

additive only: it writes new Contact Points rows and does not blank, rewrite, or

consolidate anything on the Artist rows (the 081 fill-only rule, applied to this lane).

3. Move the lookup. find_artist in

dc-canon/services/event-gate/booking_sweep.py:226 currently tries Skedda User ID →

Email (exact) → Name (always ambiguous). The email rung becomes "any Contact Points

row whose Value matches", which is what makes an old relay do its recognition job. The

id rung stays first and unchanged. The name rung stays last and stays ambiguous-by-design.

4. Parse Giggster relay SMS into rows. The messages have a fixed shape (guest name +

conversation UUID + proxy number — see 098's phone-axis section), and they already arrive

on a line the SMS poller reads, so this writes a complete row with no scraper.

Out of scope — deliberately

  • - The merge pass. Proposing merges for the 42 relay-primary cards and 25 duplicate

names is follow-on work once the table exists and can show evidence for each proposal.

Merges stay human-only and irreversible; nothing in this ticket merges a card.

  • - The expiry flip. Status is written at creation and left alone until

100 establishes each platform's lifecycle.

  • - Send-lane changes. This ticket makes the desk able to find a person by any of

their contact points. Teaching the send lanes to pick a door (stable-first, reply on the

thread's own relay) is a separate change against the message queue.

Constraints

  • - Fill-only holds — never blank a populated Alarm Display Name or Alarm Code (081).
  • - No card is auto-merged (098).
  • - Coordinate with 091: code_mirror reads these same

Artist rows daily and writes to them; land the back-fill when it is not mid-pass, and

confirm the new relation does not change what job D reads.

  • - booking_sweep runs live every 30 min. The lookup change ships as an event-gate

redeploy and gets a dry-run pass read before it goes unattended — the same order

082 used, which caught two faults no test found.

  • - Notion query-data-sources SQL can transiently omit rows — never treat a single query

as proof the back-fill covered everything; cross-check with a second signal.

Acceptance

  • - The data source exists, with normalization rules written down.
  • - Every live Artist row's existing email(s) and phone(s) are represented, including the 19

Known Emails addresses; the back-fill's own coverage count is verified twice, not once.

  • - find_artist resolves a person from any of their contact points — proven by a real

case: a card whose primary email is an old relay is found by that relay, and the same

human is found by their Skedda email, with no second card minted.

  • - A dry-run sweep pass over the live board shows no new residue attributable to the change.
  • - Known Emails is documented as retired — no lane reads it, and no habit writes to it.

the fault shape that would matter here is a lookup that stops finding people and starts

raising a row per booking, and that is not happening.

✅#015✂️Read every message we ever sent — and found some customers got the same text twice2026-08-19
  • DONE. Two leftover jobs on this ticket, and neither one turned out to be what we thought
  • First job: a tool that compared a robot's rough draft with the final message, to teach it to write better
  • That tool was scrapped back in July, because robots filled in the draft box and left the real box empty — so some messages went out blank
  • Junyan ruled: leave it dead. The new suggest-and-rewrite way of working already teaches the robots the same lesson, in a better place
  • Second job: a checkup hunting for messages accidentally recorded twice
  • We read all 9,465 messages the studio has ever sent or received — every single one, not a sample
  • The old plan for that checkup was junk: it just asked 'same words?', so every weekly reminder looked like a mistake. About 500 false alarms
  • Good news: our record book is clean. Nothing was written down twice
  • Bad news, and this is the real find: some customers actually got the same text twice
  • On June 21, three renters each got the same long message twice at 12:01, then twice again at 13:00 — four copies each in one hour
  • Two more people got doubled texts in May, and one person's reply got recorded twice in August
  • Nothing is watching for this. The outbox shows one row per message, so a double send looks perfectly normal from there
  • So we're building a checkup that hunts for double sends instead (087), and a separate job to find out what caused June 21 (088)
  • One more lesson, learned twice the hard way this session: a search that doesn't say how much it actually looked at is worthless
  • Our first search read only 346 of the 9,465 messages — 4% — and cheerfully reported everything was fine

Concierge pass to J28 silently loses SMS.

that motivated "move the ledger off git" was Zap concurrency; post-024 the poller is the

single (retry-rebase) writer, so git stays the transport. This ticket's remaining content is

Resolution (2026-08-19)

Both remaining items resolved — one retired by ruling, one graduated with its premise

corrected by evidence. Neither was built as specified, and in both cases the ticket's own

framing turned out to be the thing that needed fixing.

Item 1 — drafted-vs-sent diff: RETIRED (Junyan's ruling)

Not rebuilt. Two independent reasons, the first already settled in canon before this session:

  1. 1. The mechanism was deleted for cause on 2026-07-29. Drafted Body was removed because

agents filled it and left Message Body — the field that actually sends — empty, so those

rows sent nothing. HOUSE-RULES states the consequence plainly: the wording-diff is *retired,

not broken*. A learning field that silently blanks customer messages is not worth keeping.

  1. 2. 018's loop already carries the signal, better placed.

PROPOSE → REVIEW → EXECUTE → VERIFY makes the reviewer's rewrite the feedback: the diff

between proposed and rewritten text files the SOP fix and resets the class's promotion

counter. Rebuilding a second wording-diff now would duplicate the loop's core mechanism

weeks before 053 lands.

Junyan ruled retire (2026-08-19). Recorded so no later pass re-reports its absence as a gap:

a human noticing a wording problem remains the only path to an HTR change, filed as an

ordinary Sweep Feedback row. The banked "snapshot at send time in the send engine" option

stays available if the loop's rewrite capture proves insufficient — it is not owed by this

ticket.

Item 2 — the deep per-artist dedup check: premise inverted, graduated as 087

Ticket 013 handed this over as "the skipped check must run against the J28 ledger post-cutover."

Running it revealed the check was aimed at the wrong thing. Full scan, 9,465 records, 100%

coverage — evidence:

  • - As specified it is noise. Body-match with no time bound: 511 hits, almost all

legitimate recurring notices and weekly booking templates. Same body ≠ same message. This

is invariant G's disease exactly (045).

  • - The ledger is sound. Zero duplicate groups span more than one source at any window

(15 min / 1 h / 24 h), across 7 capture sources. Idempotency-by-msg_id verified on the

wire, per 064's rule — not assumed from the design note.

  • - The real defect is duplicate SENDS, and nothing watches for them. Same-second identical

outbound: three renters received four copies each of a 2,261-character message inside one

hour on 2026-06-21, plus doubled sends 2026-05-28 and 2026-05-30. No queue-side or

projection-side check can see this — the 📤 queue holds one row per send, so a doubled

dispatch reads as correct from there. This is why it cannot fold into

061, which watches the projection.

Junyan ruled: build the duplicate-send check → 087

(Provisional on 046's lane), and diagnose the incident

separately → 088. Detection is not repair; the Vicky

lockout is the precedent.

The finding that outlived both items

A scan that does not assert its own coverage is indistinguishable from a scan that found

nothing — and this session produced two clean-looking results over partial stores before

the real one. First the local dc-canon clone is sparse (messages/ limited to four days)

and a partial clone (blob:none), so the first pass read 346 files — 3.7% of the

store — and reported zero duplicates. Then the corrected scanner truncated silently:

git cat-file --batch emits <oid> missing for unfetched blobs, a two-field line the parser

breaked on, yielding "8,644 parsed, 0 unparsed" over 91% of the store with the shortfall

invisible. Both caught only by checking the record count against git ls-tree.

That is this map's recurring pathology reproduced inside its own diagnostic — the dead

invariants checker serving a five-day-old snapshot as health, the PBF sweep reporting

found · deduped truthfully and forever. Carried into 087 as a build requirement: the

check reports records read and span covered, and fails loudly rather than passing when it

cannot read the whole window.

Already moot / recorded elsewhere: consumer repoint (done inside 025's port), write-path

cutover (pollers + log_drop own all writes), backfill (executed in 006), the

Correspondence-Log freeze (ruled VOID 2026-07-23).

The ruling: walked through the four chronically-dropped jobs (JOB 3 reconcile, JOB 4 handback,

JOB 31 shift mirror, HEAVY-PM's JOB 33/0/5/7), Junyan ruled all four essential — *"they all

seem really important; when they are missed they each cause problems."* The right-size-by-deletion

option is dead. This is a capacity problem, and the fix is the one this ticket already owns:

take the mechanical work off the LLM pass.

The sizing read (canon, this session):

  • - JOB 31 — fully mechanical, as lane 3 already says. Query claimed rows, copy the row's own

learning loop is already retired (the Drafted Body diff field was deleted 2026-07-29). The one

prose-shaped step — the orphan-gate audit's "Current Block names a staff task" — has a structured

equivalent the tick already uses on the new board: Position = Gate:* with no open linked Action

row and no Pending-Review queue row = orphan. Rule: where the structured read is ambiguous, the

code flags the row for an agent instead of deciding — same contract as unverifiable.

  • - JOB 4 — split. Detection is mechanical and is exactly the tick's shape: Processed Action

rows, new images on parked tickets, new #desk-drops messages past the cursor. Resumption —

re-walk the SOP from the first unchecked step, branch on returned facts, read screenshots — is

genuine judgment and stays with an agent, but fed from a flag queue instead of self-scanning.

  • - HEAVY-PM (JOB 33/0/5/7) — judgment, stays LLM. Untouched here; the payoff is that the freed

budget reaches it.

The principle, stated once: code finds the work, the LLM only does flagged work. Starvation

exists because scanning competes with judging inside one budget. Move every scan to the tick and

the LLM pass starts from a worklist — it cannot starve on finding, only on doing, and a growing

flag queue is visible in a way a skipped scan never was.

New lanes:

  1. 5. JOB 3 mechanical reconcile → the event-gate tick (10-min cadence beside /loop).

Ambiguity flags, never guesses. Reporting per the standing rule — a zero pass reports zero.

  1. 6. JOB 4 detection layer → the same tick. Emits "ready to resume" flags (a view or field the

agent pass consumes). Resumption stays agentic and comes off the Concierge's self-scan.

(JOB 1 remainder deletion) unchanged. 035's test binds every lane: the retirement is done when the

✅#058🔑Can we hand out door codes ourselves? Yes — we already can2026-08-19
  • The old belief: the alarm system only lets us LOOK, never change anything, so every door-code problem had to wait for a person
  • That was never true. We can create a person, give them a code, and switch it on across all four door panels — and every change is double-checked afterwards
  • Better still, we can set the code to work only during that person's booking, instead of handing out the master code that opens everything, always
  • Checked again today: Hannah, Sharon and Hanano all have working codes on all four panels
  • The one thing we genuinely cannot do is DELETE someone. That stays a by-hand job in the Alarm.com website
  • So the trick is to never make a spare person in the first place — reuse an empty one that's already there
  • The old wrong note has been corrected where the robots read it, so nobody parks a locked-out renter on this again
Technical notes

Find a write path for Alarm.com codes — a lockout is worse than the write risk

Question

Graduated from 018. PIN generation is human-only across every code SOP

because alarm-mcp is read-only by design, which makes every access-code issue park on a staff

gate. Junyan named the risk asymmetry plainly: *"Alarm.com does not have a write API, but I want to

find a solution around this as it's not a high risk to write in the code. It's high risk if someone

is not able to get in."*

The Vicky Yeung lockout (035) is the standing evidence: detection

worked, and a renter still could not get in. 042's rung 4 —

sending the Alarm.com Emergency Code — exists only because the safer per-renter, time-boxed user is

impossible without a write path. That code is a master key (All locations / At All Times /

Existing + Future), so a real write path is also the way to stop reaching for it.

Investigate and report:

  • - what Alarm.com actually exposes — documented API, partner/dealer API, or nothing
  • - browser automation from event-gate as the fallback (the Codex in-app-browser precedent from

013b was ruled too fragile for unattended fires — say whether a

server-side headless path is different in kind, or the same trap)

  • - whether a per-renter, time-boxed user can be created at all, which is the outcome that matters
  • - credential handling and blast radius — write access to the alarm system is the most dangerous

capability on the desk; propose the containment before proposing the path

Findings land as a linked asset. The build, if there is one, is a separate ticket.

Opening step added 2026-08-07 by 063 — do this FIRST.

This ticket is built on two claims that have never been probed at the wire: *"Alarm.com has no

write API" and "the PIN is not retrievable — Alarm.com masks it"*. Both were read off tool

descriptions, and 063 found that class of claim false or overstated 6 times out of 7. Canon now

marks both UNTESTED. Probe them against the MFA-gated wire with the Mac alarm CLI before

designing any workaround — the workaround may not be needed, and if it is, the probe is what

proves it. Record the evidence either way; that closes two rows of 065.

---

Answer

CLOSED 2026-08-19 (Junyan's call: "there is already a way to add the door codes"). No workaround

was ever needed. Both founding claims are refuted, and the ticket's real product is the narrow limit

that survived.

Probed at the wire this session (read-only, business alarm connector):

  • - find_user_codes("Rupnarain") → user 247807666, code 4547, panels_covered 4,

is_access_paused false, code_is_stale false.

  • - find_user_codes("Hannah Cho") → user 247807749, code 6284, 4 panels.
  • - find_user_codes("Hanano") → user 247807667, code 7318, 4 panels.

Answering the four investigation points:

  1. 1. What Alarm.com exposes — alarm-mcp itself carries the writes: create_user, set_user_code,

grant_user_access, set_user_access_window, all confirm=True-gated and read-back verified.

The premise "read-only by design" was wrong; canon corrected 2026-08-10 in

the-loop.md, Limits register corrected today.

  1. 2. Browser automation as fallback — moot, not attempted. There is no fragile path to weigh

because the guarded API path exists.

  1. 3. Can a per-renter, time-boxed user be created? — Yes, and it has been. Hannah Cho: window

08:00 Aug 16 → 00:00 Aug 17 for a 14:00–16:00 booking. Sharon Rupnarain: 08:00 Aug 17 → 00:00

Aug 18 for 16:00–18:00. This is the outcome the ticket said mattered, so

042's rung 4 no longer has to reach for the master

Emergency Code (All locations / At All Times) to solve an access gap.

  1. 4. Containment / blast radius — already built into the surface: every write needs confirm=True,

every write is verified by read-back, and the access window is scoped to one booking rather than

permanent. The 2026-08-10 ruling classes the lane Fast track + Auto-execute with a

notify-after SMS, and requires two verification reads (find_user_codes +

check_access_covers), re-read on a later pass — because an inline pass has been observed

not to hold.

The limit that is real, and now recorded: there is no delete. Nothing removes a user or a

code slot, so duplicates and inert shells are hand-work in the Alarm.com UI. The mitigation is at

source — re-use an existing codeless shell (set_user_code + grant_user_access) instead of

creating a new user, the pattern used on Hanano I. rather than minting her a sixth.

✅#081🤖Build The Registrar — the program that sets up new bookings by itself2026-08-19
  • It is a plain program, not an AI. It reads the booking calendar itself — not the emails, because we learned the emails' 'read' mark lies
  • Junyan's rule: anyone who makes an instant booking gets the treatment. So it picks out real renter bookings by what the calendar itself says they are — not by guessing who is staff
  • We also found a trap while checking: if the program's calendar login loses its powers, every booking looks blank and the old version would have quietly skipped them all. Now it shouts 'I cannot see properly' instead
  • For each new booking it sets up the renter's profile, their door code, and a confirmation held for someone to read before it goes out
  • It never remembers which bookings it already did. Every half hour it just asks 'what is missing?' and fills the gaps — so it cannot lose its place, and nothing gets done twice
  • Bookings happening today or tomorrow are left completely alone: a different robot already owns those and is faster. If that one fails, this one writes it on the list rather than barging in
  • If our records and the door panel disagree about someone's code, our records win — it puts our code back on the panel and checks the door actually opens. It never just trusts the paperwork and texts the code out; that is how someone got sent a code that did not work
  • If two door-panel entries share one name, it stops and tells you which one to keep (the one matching our records) and which to delete — it cannot pick between two people with the same name by itself
  • The board card still goes up 2 days before the booking, same as Junyan does it now
  • We taught it to say 'I could not read the calendar' out loud, because a broken read used to look exactly like a quiet day
  • NOT switched on yet, on purpose — it writes real door codes, so Junyan turns it on himself after watching a practice run
Technical notes

Build The Registrar (booking sweep) — code chain, Actions rows for the judgment residue

Goal

Build the direct-booking one-shot lane as ruled in 079:

a Cloud Scheduler job hitting a deterministic service (event-gate/fbs-monitor style, or

its own small service in dc-canon/services/), every 30 minutes, 24/7. No LLM in the hot

path. The Host's orbit owns it conceptually (one owner for the booking lifecycle), but the

executor is code.

The chain, per pass

  1. 1. Diff the Skedda board (skedda-mcp reads, not mail — mail is pure echo; the

read/unread bit is a corrupted signal and is not consumed). Track processed bookings in

the service's own state, not Gmail.

  1. 2. Skip-class test — deterministic: AAP/subscription tags, holder = Dance Annex /

info@danceannex.ca, staff-made, FBS/monitored. NOTE from 078: only 2 of 10 holders

carried tags — the skip test must fall back to holder identity, not assume tags exist.

  1. 3. Artist record — exact-match on email first. Fuzzy name-only match ⇒ residue row,

never auto-merge (the "Julie Marouze [Gmail]" duplicate is the cautionary case).

  1. 4. Alarm access — the 079 ladder via POST /provision on alarm-mcp: (a) match ⇒ pass;

(b) dormant code on file ⇒ re-provision it; (c) no code ⇒ mint + write back; (d)

ambiguous ⇒ residue row. Reuse codeless shells before creating (Hanano rule). This is

the SINGLE WRITER for provisioning — nothing else provisions unattended.

  1. 5. Phone contact — create-if-missing on phone.com.
  2. 6. Booking confirmation — strictly templated, queued per the message-queue rules

(Pending Review unless the class is an authorized auto-send).

  1. 7. FBS board row at T−2 days — due-dated step: created on the pass where the booking

crosses 2-days-out (Junyan's existing convention; keeps the Host's board clean).

  1. 8. Tier messages — NOT this job's work. The 04:00 Host batch keeps queueing HTA/AVA/EOB

day-of/day-before. This job guarantees the prerequisites exist when the Host looks.

Every judgment case raises an Actions to Perform row (deterministic escalation); the

Host/human reads the residue. Every pass reports its own result including zero — silence

past a window is a defect (001's produces/verify_within).

Constraints carried from 079

  • - Completeness-test semantics: each pass asks "what should exist by now and doesn't",

idempotently — a step can come due later and is done on the pass where it comes due.

  • - Single-writer per surface: this job is the only unattended provisioner and the only

unattended creator of FBS rows. The Codex collision is the incident of record.

  • - Seam with the last-minute flow (today/tomorrow trigger, RUNTIMES.md): that flow is a

prototype of this chain — decide whether this job absorbs it or defers to it inside the

T−1 window. Do not run both against the same booking.

  • - Kill switch env var, own its own 🚥 Run Monitor row, and a RUNTIMES.md entry on ship.

Acceptance

  • - Service deployed + scheduled; a new direct booking is fully processed unattended within

one pass, with residue rows for the judgment cases and a per-pass report.

  • - Plumbing Map entry (day-sheet/plumbing.html) and RUNTIMES.md updated.

Reach — verified 2026-08-18, all three surfaces are plain HTTP (no MCP needed)

The question "can a non-LLM job reach the alarm and phone connectors?" has a better answer

than yes: it does not use the MCP connectors at all. MCP is the agent's transport.

event-gate — a deterministic service with no LLM — already calls every surface this job

needs directly, so the pattern is proven in production, not hypothesised:

| Surface | How code reaches it | Proven by |

|---|---|---|

| Alarm | POST /provision, /panel-states on alarm-mcp — plain HTTP routes beside the MCP server, Authorization: Bearer $ALARM_PANEL_TOKEN | alarm_watch.py reads /panel-states every minute |

| Phone | phone.com REST API directly (api.phone.com/v4/...), Bearer $PHONECOM_API_TOKEN — the phonecom-mcp connector is bypassed entirely | poller.py, actuator.py |

| Skedda | venue session cookie, SKEDDA_COOKIE or Secret Manager | skedda_read.py |

| Notion | REST, Bearer $NOTION_TOKEN | alarm_watch.py row-raising |

/provision already implements most of the 079 access ladder in code — this is the single

biggest reason the deterministic ruling is cheap to build:

  • - verify_only: true — preflight: does this person already have working access? Returns

verdict/action without writing. This is step (a), and it is what stops a second user being

created for someone already provisioned.

  • - Explicit code param ⇒ step (b), re-provisioning a dormant code read from the Artist DB.
  • - Omitted code ⇒ step (c): generates a 4-digit code, avoiding both collisions with codes

that name already holds and trivially-guessable repeats.

  • - ambiguous / match_count > 1 ⇒ a verdict the caller must escalate — step (d), the

residue row.

  • - Handles the grant-sync wait internally (12 × 5s), the trap that makes naive callers write

a window that vanishes.

  • - Windows are retired for renters (canon doorman-access-lifecycle.md): omit both bounds

and the user is permanent-until-removed; the Doorman's retirement sweep does the

time-bounding. So this job writes NO windows — matching what was done by hand for Julie

and Vicky.

Gap to close in the build — RULED 2026-08-18 by Junyan: create new users freely; do NOT

build shell-reuse. Codeless shells are visible in the Alarm.com UI and he clears them by

hand, so the litter itself is accepted. But the leftover shells are not merely cosmetic and

the build must still handle them, in a different way:

/provision resolves a name via _verdict_of, which returns ambiguous whenever the name

matches more than one record — and a codeless shell IS such a match. That is the (d) branch,

the residue row. Left unhandled, a single stale shell makes every later pass escalate a renter

whose access is fine, and check_access_covers returns manual_multiple, which blocks the

HTA alarm-code gate for that person. This is exactly what happened to Hanano I. (five shells)

and Hannah Cho (three).

So the required fix is IGNORE, not REUSE: when resolving matches for a name, discard

records with no effective code and panels_covered == 0 before counting. A codeless shell

opens no door, so it is not a second identity and must not make anyone ambiguous. Two records

that BOTH carry working codes stay ambiguous — that is the real conflict (Sharon's 8206/4547)

and must still reach a human. Preferred home is _verdict_of inside /provision, so every

caller — this job, the Doorman, the last-minute flow — gets it at once.

Cleanup owner ruled 2026-08-18: the Doorman reports shells, a human deletes them. The

sweep job ignores shells so it is never blocked; the Doorman gains a duty to surface them so

they do not accumulate invisibly. This is deliberately the same shape as its existing B(b3)

retirement sweep — Human-confirmed gate, capped per pass, humans do the actual deleting

(no delete tool exists on alarm-mcp, by design).

Spec for that duty (a Doorman prompt amendment, in its own right — see below):

  • - Sweep for users with no effective code AND panels_covered == 0.
  • - Raise ONE consolidated Access/PIN row per pass listing them, not one row per shell.

The Aug 16 hand-raised row is the format to copy: names + user ids + which id to KEEP.

  • - Never include a user who holds a working code — that is a different, more urgent class.
  • - Dedup against an open row from a previous pass; do not re-raise weekly.

✅ APPLIED 2026-08-18 — Doorman prompt v6. Junyan approved in-session; the amendment shipped

as B(b5) on trig_01XyrVCbCrSbFfWjxo9FZtxN by whole-prompt replace, verified by read-back

((b5) present; A1–A6 and B(a)/(a2)/(b)/(b2)/(b3)/(b4)/(c) intact; cron 0 8,10 *, enabled

state and all four connectors untouched). RUNTIMES.md carries the v6 entry. Scoped not to

overlap (b4): code_mirror judges the Artist DB and sees only people with an Artist row; (b5)

judges the panel's user list and sees everyone. First run: the 2026-08-18 morning pass.
